2017JOSS….2..222G
.

Appendix A
Handling Tensor Reductions

Throughout our tail calculations, we encounter cuts which are
functions of the loop momenta
\ell_{x}
, the radiation frequency

\omega
and the Euclidean metric
\delta^{op}
with four Euclidean
indices contracted against the quadrupoles,

\mathcal{N}^{ij;mn}(\{\ell_{x},\omega\})I^{ij}(\omega)I^{mn}(-\omega)\ ,

(137)

that will require tensor reduction to reach the final state factor

\kappa(\omega)=I^{ij}(-\omega)I^{ij}(\omega)
. While we could
perform this reduction term-by-term at the level of the individual
numerator and propagator combinations that appear, this order of
processing delays the introduction of
\ell^{2}
that should be zeroed
by on-shell conditions in the construction of the cut. We will
instead introduce a generic tensor reduction scheme that can easily be
applied during the process of cut assembly by constructing a tensor

\mathbf{U}^{ij;mn}
such that

\mathcal{N}^{ij;mn}I^{ij}I^{mn}=\mathcal{N}^{ij;mn}\mathbf{U}^{ij;mn}\kappa(\omega)\ ,

(138)

subject to the symmetry and trace constraints

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}^{ij;mn}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{U}^{ji;mn}=\mathbf{U}^{ij;nm}\ ,

(139)

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}^{ii;mn}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{U}^{ij;mm}=0\,.

(140)

With the spatial Euclidean metric as the only available (even-parity)
tensor to construct
\mathbf{U}
from, we find a unique object

\mathbf{U}^{ij;mn}=-2\frac{\delta^{ij}\delta^{mn}}{(d+2)d(d-1)}+\frac{\delta^{im}\delta^{jn}+\delta^{in}\delta^{jm}}{(d+2)(d-1)}\,.

(141)

We can then insert
eq.

138
during the evaluation of a cut,
after splitting the four-momenta into frequencies and spatial momenta.

We have explicitly checked that this method reproduces the
term-by-term reduction method for a number of integrals relevant to
the tails.

Appendix B
Evaluating Basis Integrals

B.1
Analytic Evaluation and Bubble Iteration

The two most important integrals we need for evaluating all of the
basis integrals appear in Chapter 10 of Smirnov’s
Analytic Tools
for Feynman Integrals

Smirnov:2012gma
. Important to note is
that Ref.
Smirnov:2012gma
works in mostly-minus Minkowski
signature, whereas the integrals we need to evaluate are in Euclidean
signature. To compensate for this, we need to Wick rotate the
Minkowski integrals to Euclidean signature via
\ell_{0}\to i\ell_{E}

which takes
\ell^{2}\to-\ell_{E}^{2}
and

\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{d}\ell\to i\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{d}\ell_{E}
. The
i
from the change in
measure cancels against the
i
as part of the
i\pi^{d/2}

normalization, and the change-in-sign of the propagators will induce
an extra phase
(-1)^{\lambda}
for each propagator, as well as

change the relative sign
between
\ell^{2}
and
m^{2}
for the
massive propagators. For example, the massive one-propagator
integral, the “tadpole”, in Minkowski signature is

\int\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{d}k}{(-k^{2}+m^{2})^{\lambda}}=i\pi^{d/2}\frac{\Gamma(\lambda-d/2)}{\Gamma(\lambda)}\frac{1}{(m^{2})^{\lambda-d/2}}\,.

(142)

Switching to Euclidean signature, we get

\int_{E}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{d}k_{E}}{(-k_{E}^{2}-m^{2})^{\lambda}}=(-1)^{\lambda}\pi^{d/2}\frac{\Gamma(\lambda-d/2)}{\Gamma(\lambda)}\frac{1}{(m^{2})^{\lambda-d/2}}\,.

(143)

From here on, we drop the explicit
E
label on the integrated
momenta. The scale of the tadpole integrals that actually occurs
throughout the basis integrals used in
section

4
is really
the graviton frequency
\omega^{2}
, and always appears with the opposite
sign compared with an actual mass as in
eq.

143
. In
addition, we need to switch to the physical
\pi
normalization. Thus,
the integral we need for evaluation is

F^{(1)}(\lambda;\omega^{2})=G^{(1)}(\lambda)=\int_{E}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{1}{(-k^{2}-(-\omega^{2}))^{\lambda}}=\frac{\Gamma(\lambda-d/2)}{\Gamma(\lambda)}\frac{(-1)^{\lambda}(4\pi)^{-d/2}}{(-\omega^{2})^{\lambda-d/2}}\,,

(144)

where we have supressed the explicit
i0
component of
\omega

because
we are not integrating over it as part of
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{d}k
.

Similarly, the
m_{1}=m_{2}=m,m_{3}=0
two-loop three-propagator integral in
Euclidean signature is given by

\displaystyle\int_{E}

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{d}k\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{d}l}{(-k^{2}-m^{2})^{\lambda_{1}}(-l^{2}-m^{2})^{\lambda_{2}}[-(k+l)^{2}]^{\lambda_{3}}}

\displaystyle=\big(\pi^{d/2}\big)^{2}(-1)^{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}}\frac{\Gamma(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{3}-d/2)\Gamma(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}-d/2)\Gamma(d/2-\lambda_{3})}{\Gamma(\lambda_{1})\Gamma(\lambda_{2})}

\displaystyle~\times\frac{\Gamma(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}-d)}{\Gamma(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+2\lambda_{3}-d)\Gamma(d/2)(m^{2})^{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}-d}}

\displaystyle=(\pi^{d/2})^{2}(-1)^{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}}B_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3};d}(m^{2})^{-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}+d}\,.

(145)

As in the case of the tadpole, the basis integrals we encounter have
the opposite relative sign between
k^{2}
and
\omega^{2}
, meaning
instead we are interested in

G^{(2)}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})=(4\pi)^{-d}(-1)^{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}}B_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3};d}(-\omega^{2})^{-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}+d}\,.

(146)

The generic bubble integral is also useful as an intermediate step, namely

\displaystyle\int_{E}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{1}{(-k^{2})^{\lambda_{1}}[-(q-k)^{2}]^{\lambda_{2}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{d/2}}{(4\pi)^{d/2}}\frac{\Gamma(d/2-\lambda_{1})\Gamma(d/2-\lambda_{2})\Gamma(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}-d/2)}{\Gamma(\lambda_{1})\Gamma(\lambda_{2})\Gamma(d-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})(-q_{E}^{2})^{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}-d/2}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{d/2}(4\pi)^{-d/2}A_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2};d}(-q_{E}^{2})^{-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}+d/2}\,.

(147)

Importantly, the Euclidean bubble produces a new Euclidean propagator,
and matching the sign choice for this new propagator to the one used
for integration absorbs the ubiquitous phase

(-1)^{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}
. Note that, as expected from the
topology, the expression is symmetric in
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
.

With these ingredients, we can begin evaluating the higher-loop basis
integrals recursively. The first non-trivial integral we need to
evaluate is the TT bulk contact integral,
F^{(3)}(1,1,1,1,0,0)
from

eq.

49
. The two potential mode propagators can be
integrated together as a bubble using
eq.

147
with

q=\ell_{1}+\ell_{3}
, resulting in a single new potential mode
propagator. The remaining integral is now of the form

eq.

145
, with a shifted index on the scaleless propagator.
Putting everything together, we have

\displaystyle F^{(3)}(1,1,1,1,0,0)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{d/2}(4\pi)^{-d/2}A_{1,1;d}G^{(2)}(1,1,2-d/2)

\displaystyle=(4\pi)^{-3d/2}A_{1,1;d}B_{1,1,2-d/2;d}(-\omega^{2})^{-4+3d/2}\,.

(148)

This expression is readily expandable near
d=3
. Evaluating the TTT
and T
4
bulk contacts proceeds in a similar manner, just with more
levels of bubble iteration, yielding

\displaystyle F^{(4)}(\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:ttt-2}})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{d}(4\pi)^{-d}A_{1,1;d}A_{1,2-d/2;d}G^{(2)}(1,1,3-d)

\displaystyle=(4\pi)^{-2d}A_{1,1;d}A_{1,2-d/2;d}B_{1,1,3-d;d}(-\omega^{2})^{-5+2d}\ ,

(149)

\displaystyle F^{(5)}(\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:tttt-7}})

\displaystyle=(4\pi)^{-5d/2}A_{1,1;d}A_{1,2-d/2;d}A_{1,3-d;d}B_{1,1,4-3d/2;d}(-\omega^{2})^{-6+5d/2}\,.

(150)

B.2
Evaluation of
Figure

8(h)

We do not know of a way to analyitcally evaluate the T
4
integral
corresponding to the
\mathcal{M}_{4}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{4}
topology,

fig.

8(h)
. However, we can exploit the fact that, like all
the other integrals we consider, the
\omega^{2}
scale of the integral
is completely factorizable

F^{(5)}(\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:tttt-8}})\equiv(-\omega^{2})^{5d/2-7}\mathcal{I}_{4\otimes 4}(1)\,,

(151)

so that we just need to numerically determine
\mathcal{I}_{4\otimes 4}(1)
as
an expansion in the dimensional regularization parameter. We use the
program AMFlow
Liu:2022chg
in conjunction with Kira

Klappert:2020nbg
to evaluate
\mathcal{I}_{4\otimes 4}(1)
up
to
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})
with 500 digits of precision at each
order. We can then use the PSLQ algorithm
Bailey:1999nv
;
pslq

to reconstruct the transcendental numbers, using the transcendental
numbers appearing in the other T
4
integrals as a guide for guessing
the basis. We find, using AMFlow’s definition of

d=3-2\epsilon
instead of the
d=3+\epsilon
that we use in the
rest of the paper,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{4\otimes 4}(1)=\frac{1}{8(4\pi)^{5}}\Bigg[

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{16+5(\log(\pi)-\gamma_{E})}{\epsilon}

\displaystyle+\big(184+80(\log(\pi)-\gamma_{E})+\frac{25}{2}(\log(\pi)-\gamma_{E})^{2}+\frac{47}{2}\zeta_{2}\big)

\displaystyle+\epsilon\Big(1888+920(\log(\pi)-\gamma_{E})+200(\log(\pi)-\gamma_{E})^{2}+\frac{125}{6}(\log(\pi)-\gamma_{E})^{3}

\displaystyle\ +408\zeta_{2}-\frac{611}{3}\zeta_{3}+\frac{235}{2}\zeta_{2}(\log(\pi)-\gamma_{E})\Big)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{2}\Big(18544+9440(\log(\pi)-\gamma_{E})+2300(\log(\pi)-\gamma_{E})^{2}

\displaystyle\ +\frac{1000}{3}(\log(\pi)-\gamma_{E})^{3}+\frac{625}{24}(\log(\pi)-\gamma_{E})^{4}

\displaystyle\ +5092\zeta_{2}+2040\zeta_{2}(\log(\pi)-\gamma_{E})+\frac{1175}{4}\zeta_{2}(\log(\pi)-\gamma_{E})^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{42193}{40}\zeta_{2}^{2}-\frac{9872}{3}\zeta_{3}-\frac{3055}{3}\zeta_{3}(\log(\pi)-\gamma_{E})\Big)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{3})\Bigg]\,.

(152)

We have verified the reconstruction using an additional numerical
evaluation in AMFlow to 1000 digits. This depth in the
\epsilon

expansion is more than sufficient, after matching
\epsilon

conventions, to obtain up through the
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0})
part of
the T
4
effective action.
